**6.3 CT1-CT4 from I^w(K) [DERIVED given I^w(k)].**
- *CT1.* <exp(w . s)>_Tor = exp(E(w)) [int r_K^w dW] / [int r_K^0 dW]. |int r_K^w dW| <= sup |r_K^w| <= exp(C |T_K|), from I^w(K). And int r_K^0 dW >= exp(-C |T_K|), from the ball |log W| <= g_K. So |<exp(w . s)>| <= exp(Re E(w) + c), with c = 2 C |T_K| depending on a_K, which is fixed, for **all** w in the class and not only the pattern. Since the left side does not change under g, CT1 holds for E_sym, by the geometric mean.
- *CT2.* E_sym(w o g) = E_sym(w) by construction, and E(conj w) = conj E(w), since rho^w is real for real w and analytic.
- *CT3.* By Mobius in the support of w, which is a scalar source with natural base point 0, E(w) = sum_k sum_X e_k(X; w|_X), with e_k(X; 0) = 0, |e_k(X; w)| <= C rho g_k^2 exp(-kappa |X|_k). (Check against the gate's reading: the leading term is n w_k g_k^2 per level-k variable, the quadratic part n w^2 g_k^4 / 2 is the contact term, dominated by level 0 at order a^{-4}, and the gradient part is of order Lam^2 a_k^2 g^4 per cell, which gives a^{-2}, as upper-chain Remark 3.3 expects. All three are local at each level, so they are additive on separated supports.) In E(sum w_i) - sum E(w_i) only polymers meeting two supports survive. Such a polymer has at least eta / a_k cells, so its level contributes at most C rho g_k^2 (v / a_k^4) exp(-kappa' eta / a_k). The sum over k converges with no dependence on a. So tau(v, eta) <= C rho v eta^{-4}. The bound e_k uses I^w(k)(b) at W = 1, where the white-noise expansion is the only input.
- *CT4.* On a torus of side L_T = 2^m P, Z = rho_K(1) int r_K dW. log rho_K(1) is a sum over levels of vacuum polymers at W = 1 that are the same functions on both tori, except those wrapping around Tor, at most C exp(-kappa P / a_k) per cell at level k, all of order one in the last levels. So log rho_K^{(L_T)}(1) = (L_T / P)^4 log rho_K^{(P)}(1) + O((L_T / a_*)^4). Also int r_K^{(L_T)} dW >= exp(-C (L_T / a_K)^4) and int r_K^{(P)} dW <= exp(C (P / a_K)^4). Together these give CT4, with c_F = C (a_* / a_K)^4. **Here the normalisation factors g_k^{n} are extensive at each level, and the last integral is at the fixed coupling g_K. So no log log(1/a) from the zero modes of Tor arises:** the torus at scale a_K has a bounded number of cells and a fixed coupling, so its zero-mode factor is a bounded constant. This answers the worry in gate item D2, whose mode count is at the bare coupling on a lattice of T cells. I have not compared the two counts line by line.

**6.4 Requirements on the consumer.** (a) My blocking factor L is odd and large, so the lattice is a = L^{-K} a_K, not dyadic. Tor has an even number T_K of last cells, so planes at multiples of P/2 are lattice planes. Lemma 7.2 of the upper chain needs even side and doubling at larger tori, not dyadic a. Its subsequence in a must be along L-adic values. **Request to the lead.** (b) CT1 is delivered for all w in the class, so the conjugate pattern is not needed on my side. (c) The step of the bare coupling between K and K + 1 is b log L + d_K - d_{K+1} + O(gamma-small), known only within 4 C_0(L). So if a consumer needs rho to cover half a trajectory step (gate item D4), rho must be chosen as a function of L, which is allowed: rho comes before gamma in 3.1. The step is not the 0.13 of a dyadic trajectory. (d) g_K = gamma / sqrt 2 exactly, by (T5), but g_0(K) need not be monotone in K unless C_0(L) is small against b log L. If the chain needs a monotone trajectory, then L must be taken large against C_0 by another route, or a subsequence of K used. **Request to the lead:** say whether monotonicity in K is used.

**6.5 Hostile pass on Section 6.** (a) The whole section is conditional on I^w(k). (b) In CT3, e_k(X; w) is a Mobius sum in the support of w. Put a variable s_c multiplying w on cube c. All of the analytic sector and the R polymers at W = 1 are analytic in s on the polydisc where |s_c w| <= 1 / (2 g^2), which has radius R_w = 1 / (2 rho g^2), by the same gas bounds, since g enters only through b^{-1/2} on the chart and through b in the action. So Cauchy in each s_c, with the fact that a connected polymer of the gas containing X has at least tau(X) + 1 cubes, gives |e_k(X; w)| <= C(L, M) (2 rho g^2)^{|X|} exp(-kappa d(X)), where the densities w_k(x) f_{k,x} in the exponent carry the tree norm of Lemma 4.5k, and are grouped by hull as in (EI), and for |X| = 1 the first order term n w g^2 is exact. There are no walks in this route, so nothing is left at that level. (c) Item 5 of 6.1 needs only the size of the marginal part of H^#, C(L, M) g p^3, and the summability of g^3 p^c. The earlier appeal to parity is withdrawn. (d) E must absorb the quadratic contact term, of order a^{-4}, and it does: it is in the Mobius sum at level 0, where it has range a. (e) In CT1 the constant c depends on (a_* / a_K)^4 and on gamma, through g_K, and these are fixed. So c is large but cutoff-free.

### 7. Fluctuation form: a route without random walks [labels on each step]

**Target (COER).** At a regular non-abelian background, with frozen boundary, the fluctuation form on ker Q is bounded below, and its inverse has exponentially decaying kernel, uniformly in k and the volume. Every Gaussian in (SF3), the uniqueness in (SF1), the Z-core cost and the normalisations consume it. My only source is CMP 99:389, unread; Dimock's analogues are abelian, and they use random walks with local gauge patches. The route below uses neither.

**7.1 Lower bound, abelian.** Let q_k(B) = min{ ||dA||^2 on the fine lattice : Q_k A = B }.

*Lemma 7.1.* q_k(B) >= ||d_k B||^2_{l^2(T_k)}, for every k. [DERIVED.] The curvature map is the n-fold direct average, n = L^k, by the composition rule: base-point offset and path offset written in base L biject the nested sums with the direct ones (RENEW section 2, rechecked). Its row sum is n^2 and its column sum is n^{-2}, so Cauchy-Schwarz gives the bound with constant exactly 1. Nothing compounds, since this is one-shot.

*Lemma 7.2 (block Poincare).* If QB = 0 on the bonds of a block pair, then sum_b |B_b|^2 <= C_P(L) ( sum_p |d_k B_p|^2 + sum_{x} |g(x)|^2 ), summed over the hull, where g(x) is the axial average of B from the block centre to x. [DERIVED.] An interior link equals g(x + e) - g(x) up to plaquette sums over a bounded surface. Two crossing links of the same face differ by interior links and a strip of plaquettes. The average over the face is fixed by QB = 0, in terms of interior links and h(y) = avg_x g(x). All constants depend on L only, and the statement lives on a finite graph at level k.

*Theorem 7.3 (abelian COER).* With Balaban's gauge term ||G B||^2 = sum |g|^2, the form q_k + G^* G is at least 1 / C_P(L) on ker Q, for all k, and so the fluctuation covariance is bounded by C_P(L) at each level-k link. [DERIVED from 7.1, 7.2.] Mixed bonds, with one block frozen, impose nothing on interior links by Lemma 1.1, so the same proof runs on exterior blocks; frozen variables have zero variation. **The whole lower bound is finite-graph plus one Cauchy-Schwarz.**

*Star removal.* Q_root(d Lambda) = Lambda(y') - Lambda(y), so q_k(B) = min over coarse gauge of the same problem with Q_str, in which there is no star through y. Hence q_k(B) depends on B only through d_k B: q_k(B) = min{ ||F||^2 : F closed, A_k F = d_k B }. [DERIVED.]

*Saddle stability.* In units of the block, Q_str has diagonal QQ^* weight int tri^2 = 2/3 and overlap int tri(x) tri(x+1) = 1/6 with each of two neighbours along the bond, and none across directions or transversely. So its symbol is 2/3 + (1/3) cos theta >= 1/3. [DERIVED, explicit.]

**7.2 Non-abelian form [DERIVED from Theorem G'].** In a patch gauge Hess F_{k,Omega}(V) = q_{n,Omega} + O(C(L, R) |v|), uniformly in k. So at a regular background the form is coercive on the slice as soon as eps_0 is small against C(L, R)^{-1} L^{-2}: the lower bound is linear (Lemma 7.1 and the Poincare bound of (CUT) item 1), and the correction is nonlinear. In particular the Nielsen-Olesen negative mode does not appear in the fiber at this curvature. The first version of this section needed an all-level sup norm on U, which is what (REG) was for. It is gone.

**7.3 Decay of the level-k covariance [DERIVED at trivial background; perturbation by the choice of eps_0].** On the level-k lattice, take H := Hess F_{k,Om}(V^*), with the gauge term of (CUT) item 1 and the projector Pi on ker Q^{V^*}. At the trivial background and away from holes, H has exponentially local kernel at the absolute rate delta_0 ((4.4) at level k, or the analytic symbol of 9.4 at the trivial background), and the form on ker Q has spectrum in [c L^{-2}, C], with c, C absolute. Conjugating by exp(phi), scalar, |grad phi| <= delta / L: the form changes by at most C (delta / L) ||grad B|| ||B|| + C (delta / L)^2 ||B||^2, and ||B|| <= C L ||grad B|| on ker Q, so the relative change is at most C delta. Pi is local at scale L, so its commutator is also C delta. *The gauge term [written out in this round].* Pi_0 is local at scale L, but its commutator with exp(2 phi) on D^* u brings the block means of D^* u, which the form does not contain. They are bounded all the same. For a block y, with a := 1_y and E a smooth with the same block averages, <a, D^* u> = <a - E a, Pi_0 D^* u> + <D E a, u>, since a - E a has zero block averages, and ||D E a|| <= C L^{-1} ||a||. So for u in X, ||(1 - Pi_0) D^* u||_y <= C ( ||Pi_0 D^* u||_{N(y)} + L^{-1} ||u||_{N(y)} ), and by (COER-G), ||D^* u|| is controlled by the form, locally, with a bounded weight ratio. So the commutator is relative C delta, as claimed. e^{phi} u is not in X, and neither is e^{2 phi} u: subtract l Q of it, which by Q u = 0 is at most C delta e^{2 phi} ||u||_{tube}, in norm, and C delta L^{-1} e^{2 phi} ||u||_{tube} in form, again relative C delta. Hence

    |C_k(b, b')| <= C(L) exp( - c d(b, b') / L ),    c absolute,

uniformly in k. A scalar weight commutes with Ad, so the non-abelian case has no gauge patches, and the corrections are of order C(L, R) eps_0, small against c L^{-2} by the choice of eps_0. **The rate c / L with c absolute is linear data, and that is what makes kappa = c M in (EI) free of L.**

**7.4 What failed in this section, and why.**
- *Energy-only regular class.* The first version of this section set this aside, because it seemed to need a lattice Uhlenbeck gauge. **It is what Theorem G' now does, without that gauge:** the fixed point is perturbative, the regular class is still defined by curvature, and uniqueness comes level by level from convexity. The one idea I had missed is that l^4 is exactly critical for Q_str with norm one, so the sup norm on the fine lattice was never needed.
- *Translation-invariant Laurent route.* It gives decay in the abelian case, but it does not extend to a non-trivial background or to a region with boundary. Replaced by 4.5g and 7.3.
- *Step-wise (REG):* shape factors compound as (C_s / L^2)^k. Replaced by the critical norm, in which a shape factor does not enter.
- *(COER) as the hardest inequality.* I named it hardest early on. The lower bound is short, so it was not.
- *Sign of beta by per-level analysis.* The paramagnetic second-order term has a sign at every level, because ker Q_{m-1} is inside ker Q_m and Tr(X V Y V) >= 0 for X, Y >= 0. [Derived.] The diamagnetic and constraint terms do not. So a sign per level did not come out, and it is not needed: (8.2) and Proposition 8.4.

**7.5 Hostile pass on Section 7.** (a) Lemma 7.1 is for the linearised map only. Its use at non-trivial U now goes through the analytic bound of Theorem G', with a correction proportional to |v|. (b) The gauge term of (CUT) item 1 is a device for the Gaussian; the integrand is gauge invariant. An earlier version treated its normalisation as a determinant at the Gaussian level, and did not notice that at nonlinear order it is a non-Gaussian integral over the orbit. With FP in the weight the normalisation is an exact constant, and FP is the explicit determinant, which goes into the polymers with the rest. I have not computed FP beyond its locality and size. (c) (5.5) is on real fields, and it says nothing on the complex class. Complex bounds come from analyticity of the exterior only. (d) None of the numerics tested the final map at threshold eps_0.

### 8. FLOW by telescoping: the sign without per-level evaluation

Credit: the background-field structure of the coupling recursion is Balaban's CMP 109 (1.3), (1.4), (0.20) as transcribed in NOTES section 9 (LANE-READ). The diamagnetic and paramagnetic split at constant field is Nielsen and Hughes. The telescoping below is derived here.

**8.1 Exact one-loop identity [DERIVED].** Let F_j be the classical functional, F_{j+1}(W) = min over Fib(W) of F_j(V), the tropical recursion (exact, with F_0 = A). Let V^* be the minimiser and H_L = Hess F_j(V^*) - mu . Hess M the Hessian of the Lagrangian, with mu the multiplier. By Legendre duality, Hess F_{j+1}(W) = (Q^U H_L^{-1} (Q^U)^*)^{-1}. By the Fourier representation of the delta function,

    int dB delta(Q^U B) exp(-(1/2) <B, H_L B>) = (2 pi)^{(N - d)/2} (det H_L)^{-1/2} (det Q^U H_L^{-1} (Q^U)^*)^{-1/2} = (2 pi)^{(N-d)/2} (det H_L)^{-1/2} (det Hess F_{j+1}(W))^{1/2}.

[H_L is singular on the tangents of G_{j,0}, and the coarse gauge directions need the on-shell identity, so the Legendre formula and this integral are on orbit quotients, as a reader noted; that is what the factorisation below supplies.] No separate Jacobian appears: the weights of Q^U are inside Hess F_{j+1}. On covariantly constant backgrounds mu vanishes with D^* F, so H_L = Hess F_j. Gauge directions go through the exact factorisation of the orbit integral: [dB_j / G_j] = [dW / G_{j+1}] [fiber / G_{j,0}]. *This factorization has no background-dependent Jacobian*, and that matters, since a non-telescoping local factor would add a constant to b log L at every level: by covariance of M the coarse gauge transformation is the value of h at the block centres, so G_j = G_{j+1} x G_{j,0} is a splitting of product Haar measure, with G_{j,0} the transformations with h = 1 at the centres. The device of (CUT) item 1, on zero block averages, only changes coordinates on the fiber; the one-loop term is taken from (8.1), and the device enters only in the non-Gaussian corrections, where its Faddeev-Popov factor (CUT item 1) is a local analytic functional. Define L_j(V) as the one-loop gauge-invariant integral of Hess F_j. Then

    log Z^{(j)}(U) = L_j(V) - L_{j+1}(W),                                                                                   (8.1)

so the one-step fluctuation determinant is a **difference of two copies of one functional at adjacent levels**. The sum over levels telescopes. This replaces the direct sign analysis of 7.4: the variation of the constraint is inside Hess F_{j+1}.

**8.2 The coefficient b [DERIVED, continuum, formal in the usual heat-kernel sense; the value agrees with the known one-loop coefficient].** Take SU(2), background F_12 = B_ad on the charged components, i.e. link U = exp(i theta sigma_3) with B_ad = 2 curl theta, so that the action density of A = sum_p (1 - (1/2) tr U_p) is B_ad^2 / 8. A complex scalar of charge 1 has K(t) = (16 pi^2 t^2)^{-1} (B t / sinh B t). In Feynman gauge the charged vector has spin shifts +2B, -2B and two zero shifts, and the charged ghost enters twice with the opposite sign. So with mass mu,

    X = int (dt / t) e^{-mu^2 t} K(t) [ 2 + e^{2 B t} + e^{-2 B t} - 2 ] = int (dt / t) e^{-mu^2 t} K(t) 2 cosh(2 B t),

and the B^2 term is (B^2 / 16 pi^2)(4 - 1/3) int dt / t e^{-mu^2 t}, which is (11/3)(B^2 / 8 pi^2) log(1 / (mu a)). In terms of A this is

    X = b log(1 / (mu a)) * A,    b = 11 / (3 pi^2) = 0.3715...,

the 4 being paramagnetic and the -1/3 diamagnetic. Check: in this normalisation 1/g_B^2 runs at the rate 4 N b_0 = 11 N^2 / (12 pi^2) at N = 2, as recorded in the program's Proposition LF-count.

**8.3 From (8.1) to the flow [DERIVED given the marginal coefficients below].** Let Phi_F(V) := -(1/2) log det H_F(V) + log det Gh(V) on the lattice of the level in question, with a Feynman term: H_F(V) = Hess F(V) + D_V D_V^*, Gh(V) = D_V^* D_V. Each Phi is massless and has no polymer form of its own: only differences do. Insert the Wilson form A^{(j)} on the same lattice:

    log Z^{(j)}(V^*(W)) = [Phi_{F_j} - Phi_A]^{(j)}(V^*(W)) - [Phi_{F_{j+1}} - Phi_A]^{(j+1)}(W) + WW_L(W) + X_mu(W),
    WW_L(W) := Phi_A^{(j)}(V^*(W)) - Phi_A^{(j+1)}(W).

Let d_j be the coefficient of A in [Phi_{F_j} - Phi_A]^{(j)} on slowly varying fields, that is, d_j = c_j - c_W in the notation of the earlier version; d_0 = 0 since F_0 = A. On slowly varying W, A(V^*(W)) = A(W) up to irrelevant terms, by exactness of (R4) on constant curvature. WW_L has coefficient b log L (8.7). X_mu is the term linear in the multiplier in (8.1). It has the form <mu, Y(W)>, with Y a covariant local link-valued function, and the only covariant vector of dimension at most 3 is D_nu F_{nu mu}. Both factors have dimension 3, so X_mu has dimension 6 and no marginal part: on the scaled field of Lemma 8.5 it is O(xi^2) in total. So, with g_{j+1}^{-2} = g_j^{-2} - beta_{j+1},

    beta_{j+1} = b log L + d_j - d_{j+1} + rho_j,                                                                          (8.2)

with rho_j the marginal part of the higher-order polymers, and of the recycled gas. By size alone, |rho_j| <= C_2(L, M) g_j p(g_j)^3, Proposition 11.1 applied to (EI). **I do not use any parity improvement to g^2.**

*One-loop identity at the level of a density (OL) [DERIVED at the level of each step].* In the delta function of mu_W, the Jacobian of the true map is already included: at a critical point V^* of the fiber problem, int db delta(Q^{V^*} b) exp(-(1/2) <b, H_L b>) = const (det H_L)^{-1/2} (det Q^{V^*} H_L^{-1} (Q^{V^*})^*)^{-1/2}, and the Legendre identity Hess F_{j+1}(W) = (Q^{V^*} H_L^{-1} (Q^{V^*})^*)^{-1} is exact for the true M, with its true differential, so nothing from M is left over. The same integral, by Schur, is const (det H_L|_X)^{-1/2} (det Q Q^*)^{-1/2}, with X = ker Q^{V^*}. **In this form each factor is a determinant of a local operator with a gap at fixed L, so it has a gauge-invariant exp-local density, analytic in W.** So the density is the local object, and the two massless functionals appear only in the identity: on a torus, with W smooth of amplitude e, at second order in e,

    density sum = L_j(V^*(W)) - L_{j+1}(W) + X_mu(W) = [Phi_{F_j} - Phi_A](V^*) - [Phi_{F_{j+1}} - Phi_A](W) + WW_L(W) + X_mu(W).

Take W a plane wave of small amplitude and coarse momentum Q != 0 on the torus, and let T -> infinity at fixed Q. Each term of the identity has a limit at second order in the amplitude, since on the torus the zero-mode terms are of order T^{-4} / |Q|^2. Then let Q -> 0. The left side is the symbol of a local density, so it is analytic in Q: beta (Q^2 delta - Q Q) + O(Q^4). On the right, the first two terms give d_j and d_{j+1} by Proposition 8.6, WW_L gives b log L by 8.7, and X_mu has no such term. So (8.2) holds as an identity of constants, with no extra term. The reflection in each axis other than mu fixes the bond and flips F_{mu nu}, so Y has no term linear in F, which is the classification used for X_mu. The Haar density, the Faddeev-Popov factor beyond its one-loop value, and the O(b^2) terms of M are at order g^2 and go into rho_j.

**Proposition 8.4 (what the induction consumes from the flow) [DERIVED, line by line].** Let (8.2) hold for j <= k, with |d_j| <= C_0 and |rho_j| <= C_2 g_j p(g_j)^3, **where C_0, C_2 may depend on L**, and g_j <= gamma, with gamma small against C_0, C_2, L. Put u_j := g_j^{-2}, w_j := u_j - d_j. Then:
- (T1) w_{j+1} = w_j - b log L - rho_j, and b log L + rho_j lies in [(b/2) log L, (3b/2) log L]. So **w_j is strictly decreasing, for any L > 1**, while u_j itself need not be monotone.
- (T2) |u_{j+1} - u_j| <= 2 b log L + 2 C_0, so u_{j+1} <= (1 + C gamma^2) u_j, at most (5/4) u_j, for gamma small. This is all Theorem 4.3 uses: it has a factor 3/2 in hand.
- (T3) For i <= j: u_i >= u_j + (j - i)(b/2) log L - 2 C_0 >= gamma^{-2} / 2 + (j - i)(b/2) log L.
- (T4) sum_{i <= j} g_i^3 p(g_i)^c and sum_{i <= j} exp(-p(g_i)^2 / 2) are finite, uniformly in j, and tend to zero with gamma. These are the summability that 6.1 item 6 and the mid-field shift of 9.6 ask for. [From (T3): the first sum is at most C gamma p(gamma)^c / (b log L). The second converges since 2 p_0 > 1.]
- (T5) (Trajectory.) For every K there is g_0 with g_K = gamma / sqrt 2 exactly and g_j <= gamma for all j <= K, and then 1 / g_0^2 = 2 / gamma^2 + K b log L + (d_0 - d_K) + sum_j rho_j.

*Proof of (T5).* g_K is continuous in g_0 on the set where g_j <= gamma for j < K, because each beta is a continuous function of the earlier couplings. For g_0 small, u_K is large. Lower u_0 until some u_j = gamma^{-2}, j <= K. By (T1), u_K <= u_j + 2 C_0 <= gamma^{-2} + 2 C_0 < 2 gamma^{-2} at that point. The intermediate value theorem gives u_K = 2 gamma^{-2} with all u_j >= gamma^{-2} on the way.  []

**So the demand that C_0 be free of L, which I named the hardest inequality in the last round, was not a real requirement.** It came from asking beta_{j+1} > 0 at each step. That is not consumed: gamma comes after L, so a bounded local reversal of the flow costs a relative change gamma^2 C_0 in the coupling. What is needed is that c_j stays bounded in j at fixed L, and that the coefficient of log T is exactly b at each level. Also L need not be large for (FLOW): L large is for the margins in 3.1. (T5) replaces a monotone trajectory; a ~ L^{-K} remains a function of K, and g_0(K) is what the interface receives.

**Lemma 8.5 (derivative count without Ward identities) [DERIVED given Theorem G' and Steps 1 to 5 of Proposition 11.1].** Let D := F_j - A^{(j)}. Its Taylor kernels at the trivial background have symbols, analytic in a strip, with

    |D^(2)(p)| <= C |p|^4,   |D^(3)(p, q)| <= C (|p| + |q|)^3,   |D^(4)(p, q, r)| <= C (|p| + |q| + |r|)^2,

with C depending on the constants of Theorem G', and so possibly on L, but not on j.

*Proof.* D is a sum of gauge-invariant densities with exponentially decaying sensitivity (Theorem G', 4.5g), with no F^2 term, since the second-order form is ||d_j B||^2 (1 + O(P^2)) by Lemma 10.1 and the expansion G_mu(P)^{-1} = P^2 (1 + O(P^2)). Take a fixed trigonometric polynomial f and V_xi := exp(i xi f(xi .)). Compute D(V_xi) in two ways. (1) By Steps 1 to 5 of Proposition 11.1 applied to the densities, which only needs smoothness of the explicit field with alpha = 1: per site D(V_xi) is at most C xi^5, so in total, over xi^{-4} sites, D(V_xi) = O(xi). (2) By Taylor in f and Euler-Maclaurin in the kernels, D(V_xi) = sum_{m, r} xi^{m + r - 4} P_{m,r}(f), with P_{m,r}(f) the local polynomial of degree m in f with r derivatives, whose coefficient is the degree-r Taylor coefficient at the origin of the symbol of D^(m). Comparing powers of xi for all f, and using homogeneity in f, gives P_{m,r} = 0 for m + r <= 4. By polarisation the Taylor coefficients of D^(m) of degree r vanish for m + r <= 4. The hypercubic inversion removes odd m + r. That leaves exactly the stated orders.  []

The same argument gives H_{t,1}, H_{t,2} of the expected orders. **This replaces the Ward hierarchy, and the claim in the earlier 8.4a that gauge invariance ties the low vertices to the F^2 term is now a proof.**

**Proposition 8.6 (|d_j| <= C_0(L), uniformly in j) [DERIVED given Lemma 8.5; power count written out, not all terms line by line].** In Feynman gauge the ghost is the same for F_j and for A^{(j)}, so it cancels in the difference. Put H_t(V) = H_A(V) + t delta(V), delta = Hess D. With a background of amplitude e and momentum q, expand delta = delta_0 + delta_1 + delta_2 + ..., H_t = H_0 + t delta_0 + H_{t,1} + H_{t,2} + ..., G_t := (H_0 + t delta_0)^{-1}. Since delta_0 >= 0 by Lemma 7.1, ||G_t(p)|| <= 1 / phat^2 for all t, **with a linear constant equal to 1**. Then

    second order in e of Phi_{F_j} - Phi_A = int_0^1 dt ( -(1/2) Tr[G_t delta_2] + (1/2) Tr[G_t H_{t,1} G_t delta_1] + (1/2) Tr[G_t H_{t,2} G_t delta_0] - (1/2) Tr[G_t H_{t,1} G_t H_{t,1} G_t delta_0] ).

Lemma 8.5 gives delta_0 ~ p^4, delta_1 ~ e (|p| + |q|)^3, delta_2 ~ e^2 (|p| + |q|)^2, H_{t,1} ~ e (|p| + |q|), H_{t,2} ~ e^2. By gauge invariance of the difference, Pi_{mu nu}(q) vanishes at q = 0 and is transverse; the only transverse tensor of order q^2 allowed by the hypercubic group is q^2 delta_{mu nu} - q_mu q_nu; call its coefficient d_j. For |p| >= 2 |q|, Taylor in q: the q^2 coefficient of each term is at most C e^2 / phat^2, and int d^4 p / phat^2 converges at small p; at large p all is bounded. The Taylor remainder is C q^4 / p^4 in the integrand, so it contributes O(q^4 log(1 / |q|)). For |p| <= 2 |q| each term is at most C e^2 q^4 / (p^2 (p + q)^2) or C e^2 q^2 / p^2 or C e^2 q^2 / (p + q)^2, or C e^2, and each integrates to O(e^2 q^4). So Pi_diff(q) = d_j (q^2 delta - q q) + O(q^4 log (1 / |q|)), with |d_j| <= C_0, where C_0 is the constant of Lemma 8.5 times a number depending on d. Uniform in j, because Theorem G' is. No infrared logarithm appears in the difference, so **the coefficient of log T is the same for every j**, and it is the Wilson value.

**8.7 The Wilson term [DERIVED at the level of each step; the coefficient itself is 8.2].** This is the classical comparison of a lattice one-loop integral with its continuum limit. I derive what is consumed: the coefficient of log L, with no constant.

Take second order in a background U = exp(i theta sigma_3), theta small, on Z^4 with spacing 1, as the limit T -> infinity of the torus at fixed momentum q != 0. On the torus the terms with loop momentum at p = 0 or p = -q are of order T^{-4} / |q|^2, so they vanish in this limit, and

    Pi_{mu nu}(q) = (2 pi)^{-4} int_{BZ} d^4 p I_{mu nu}(p, q),

where I is the second-order integrand of -(1/2) log det H_F + log det Gh, built from the charged Hessian of A at U (explicit: d_theta^* cos(Theta) d_theta plus a term sin(Theta) in the plaquette flux Theta, plus D_theta D_theta^*) and the charged ghost. The neutral components see theta only through cos(Theta_p): at second order in theta this is a constant times sum_p Theta_p^2, of order |q|^2 with no logarithm, so it only moves c_W. I is a trigonometric rational function with denominators phat^2 and (p + q)hat^2. Used: (i) exact lattice gauge invariance gives Pi_{mu nu}(q) qhat_nu = 0 and Pi(0) = 0, since a constant theta is pure gauge on Z^4; (ii) |d_q^n I(p, q)| <= C_n |phat|^{-2 - n} for |phat| >= 2 |qhat|, and I even under (p, q) -> (-p, -q); (iii) for small p, q, I = I_c (1 + O(|p|^2 + |q|^2)), with I_c the continuum Feynman-gauge integrand, homogeneous of degree -2. These are read off the form of H_F.

Let |q| <= Lambda << 1. Write Pi(q) = Pi(q) - Pi(0) = (2 pi)^{-4} ( int_{|p| <= Lambda} + int_{|p| > Lambda} ) [I(p, q) - I(p, 0)].

*Outer part.* Taylor in q: the odd orders integrate to zero by (ii), so the integrand is (1/2) q q d_q d_q I(p, 0) + O(|q|^4 / |phat|^6). Its second term integrates to O(|q|^4 / Lambda^2). In the first, by (iii) d_q d_q I(p, 0) = |p|^{-4} beta(p / |p|) (1 + O(|p|^2)), with beta the corresponding coefficient of I_c, so int_{|p| > Lambda} = 2 pi^2 <beta> log(1 / Lambda) + const + O(Lambda^2), with <beta> the angular average, a fixed tensor. **The const depends on the lattice, and on nothing else: it is the same at all q.**

*Inner part.* By (iii), up to a relative O(Lambda^2), which integrates to o(|q|^2) since the integrand is at most C |q|^2 / |p|^4 for |p| >= 2 |q| and at most C / |p|^2 + C / |p + q|^2 below, it is the continuum integral. By homogeneity that is |q|^2 h(qhat, Lambda / |q|), with h(qhat, R) := int_{|p| <= R} [I_c(p, qhat) - I_c(p, 0)], and h = 2 pi^2 <beta(qhat, qhat)> log R + h_0(qhat) + o(1) as R -> infinity, by the same Taylor expansion at large p.

Adding, with Lambda := |q|^{1/2}, the logarithms of Lambda cancel, and

    Pi_{mu nu}(q) = (q^2 delta_{mu nu} - q_mu q_nu) ( b log(1 / |q|) + c_W(qhat) + o(1) ),    as q -> 0,

where the tensor has this form by (i), b is fixed by <beta>, and c_W(qhat) is bounded and depends only on the direction. So at the same direction,

    Pi_1(q) - Pi_1(L q) = b log L + o(1):                                                                                  (8.3)

**the constant cancels exactly, for every L.** *The value of b.* <beta> is a coefficient of the continuum integrand at large p, so it does not change if a mass mu is added to all denominators. With a mass, the second-order term at q -> 0 is analytic, and by transversality it is the coefficient of F^2 for constant F, which is what 8.2 computes from the exact Landau spectrum. The mass only cuts off the logarithm. So b = 11 / (3 pi^2), in the normalisation of A.

WW_L(W) = Phi_A^{(j)}(V^*(W)) - Phi_A^{(j+1)}(W) compares the two lattices on the same field: V^*(W) has symbol (lift of W) at second order, and at small coarse momentum Q the lift is exp(i (xi / L) f(xi x / L)) up to irrelevant terms, by (R4) and 9.4. At momentum q = Q / L on the finer lattice and Q on the coarser, (8.3) gives the coefficient of A(W) in WW_L as exactly b log L + o(1), as Q -> 0. **It is the same for every j, and c_W is never needed.**

*Numerics, under a confirmed lock, criteria written first:* `checks/hard-core--independent_wilson_b.py`. Second order in an abelian background, exact trace over the torus, with the plaquette form h_ij = s_i s_j exp(2 i (a_i - a_j)) exp(i Theta sgn(j - i)), derived from E = prod exp(i s_i b_i) and written in the script. **The registered FAIL-WARD is against a threshold I set without computing the zero-mode term:** in fact |Phi^(2)| qhat^2 = 16.0 at every q and N run, to three digits, for a pure-gauge background of unit polarisation, which is exactly the size of the term left out with p = 0, so the Ward identity holds up to that term. The coefficient: (R(q) - R(3 q)) / log 3 at q = 0.2618 is 0.2392, 0.3438, 0.3619 at N = 24, 48, 72, against b = 0.3715: ratios 0.644, 0.926, 0.974, **PASS-B and PASS-B2**. At fixed q N the ratio is unchanged (0.64 at n = 1 for both N = 24 and N = 48), so what is seen is a finite-size effect in q N, as the limit T -> infinity at fixed q predicts. So sign and size of b log L are confirmed on this lattice Hessian, up to 3 percent, without the heat kernel of 8.2. This is a check, not an input.

**8.8 Hostile pass on Section 8.** (a) (8.1) is exact on covariantly constant backgrounds; on general slowly varying backgrounds the difference is X_mu, by the argument in 8.3. **I had written that Y has an analytic local expansion. A reader pointed out that this is false:** Y = (1/2) Tr[ (Hess F_j + D D^*)^{-1} Hess M_c ] contains massless propagators, so it is not exp local, and it has a generic Q^2 log Q at first order in the amplitude theta, and there are also terms in mu^2. The conclusion survives, by amplitude counting: Y vanishes at flat, by colour; mu is of order Q^2 theta; so <mu, Y> and mu^2 are of order Q^4 log Q theta^2, against the marginal Q^2 theta^2. So X_mu has no marginal part. (b) A torus has only quantised constant curvature. Since beta is now extracted polymer by polymer (11.1) from one-step densities on a slowly varying field, no constant curvature background is needed, and (8.1) enters only through the identity of densities. (c) The neutral sector and zero modes appear in Phi at both levels; they are the same functions of the coarse field at leading order, and they differ by irrelevant terms because the difference of densities is local. Not checked at second order through the nested means, except through Lemma 8.5, which does cover nested means, as it is a statement about F_j itself. (d) In (T5) continuity of beta in the earlier couplings needs the hard cuts of (CUT) to depend continuously on g. They do, after integration: at fixed variables the integrand is continuous in g off the null set where an indicator or a cut switches, it is dominated by the Gaussian bounds of Lemma 4.7b, uniformly for g in a compact set, and the polymer expansions converge uniformly. So each polymer is continuous in g_0, ..., g_k, at fixed W, by dominated convergence and induction, and beta(e) is by Cauchy on the polydisc. (e) (T1) needs C_2 g p^3 small against b log L, which is why gamma comes after L. If L were close to 1, that would break, but L is large anyway. (f) A real weakness: c_j is only known to be bounded, not computed, so the actual step of beta is known only within 4 C_0(L). The interface must not be told a numeric trajectory step; see 6.4. (g) Proposition 8.6 depends on Theorem G' at the trivial background only, on the whole torus, where shapes do not enter. So the list-of-shapes obligation does not touch (FLOW).

### 9. Linear lift bounds and the gauge notes

An earlier version of this section bounded the vertices of F_j with constants free of L, off the star, in the l^4 norm, with a loss L^{1/2} through the star on rough inputs. **No consumer needs L-free nonlinear constants any more** (Proposition 8.4, Text 4.5), so that bookkeeping is gone; the part that is still consumed is the following.

**9.4 Lift bounds, free of L [DERIVED given Lemmas 4.5e, 4.5g; linear; rewritten in this round].** The lift for a level-j link depends only on n = L^j, in the linear theory. Let f^* be the minimal curvature for data with coarse curvature F' at level j, so d f^* = 0 and d^* f^* = Q_str^* mu. By Lemma 4.5g, |mu(c)| <= C ||f^*||_{N(c)}, and the energy of f^* per coarse cell is at most C sup |F'|^2 by Lemma 4.5e, with a tail at rate delta in level-j units. The source Q_str^* mu is bounded by C n^{-3} sup |F'|. In a ball of radius n / C in the interior of Om, f^* is the sum of grad G * (Q_str^* mu), at most C n n^{-3} sup |F'| by |grad G(x)| <= C |x|^{-3}, and a harmonic form, whose sup is at most C n^{-2} times its l^2 norm on the ball, by the mean value property of a lattice harmonic function, in which the same bound holds up to the lattice scale. So **|f^*| <= C n^{-2} sup_x exp(-delta d(x, .)) |F'_x|**, and the same argument for differences gives the Holder modulus (|x - x'| / n)^a, a < 1, with no logarithm at the block faces since only in-plane derivatives of the tube weight enter. In the one-step case, the level-k curvature of V^*(W) is at most C_s L^{-2} sup |F'|, with Holder modulus (|x - x'| / L)^a, up to a correction C(L, R) eps_0 sup |F'|, second order in F' and vanishing at flat fields, by Cauchy on the class, negligible by the choice of eps_0, also at small F'. All with the weight exp(-delta d). Near corners of Lambda only the Morrey bound of 4.5h holds. **What was wrong.** This paragraph had a Fourier argument: 1 / G_mu(P) analytic in a strip, so mu decays. A reader pointed out that the Feynman multiplier G^{-1} B is not the multiplier of the minimal lift, since chat^* G^{-1} B != 0 when G_mu is anisotropic, and the true multiplier has a denominator chat^* G^{-1} chat of order P^4, so analyticity of 1 / G_mu says nothing about it; and that the bound |a(x)| <= C n^{-1} exp(-delta d) I had written cannot hold in Coulomb gauge, whose grad G has a tail |x|^{-3}. **Only the curvature is exponentially local,** and that is by the energy estimate of Lemma 4.5g. No consumer uses a bound on a in a fixed gauge; Lemma 4.5j has its own gauge. Also, F^* is L^{-2} K * F', with K of rate delta, and not L^{-2} F': for W rough at the coarse scale, sum_x |F^*|^2 = q_L(W), up to R = 6.45 times ||d W||^2. This is what the identification in 11.1 has to respect.

**9.6 Gauge notes and the cut.**
- *Maximal tree gauge [DERIVED].* In each L-block, based at the block centre: for a gauge-invariant integrand, setting tree links to 1 has trivial Jacobian on Haar measure, at every field, rough or not, so there is no Gribov problem. It serves for frozen rough cubes. On the small-field slice it is badly conditioned, by a factor L^2, and (CUT) item 1 is used there.
- *Two-tier fluctuation cut [DERIVED as a scheme].* Theorem 27 of Dimock I (ultralocal cluster expansion, which I read) needs H_0 small, which fails on the whole regular slice, where the cubic term is not small. So use the white-noise cut |W| <= p(g), p(g) = (log 1/g)^{p_0}, for the expansion, as Dimock does. A mid-field cell, with p(g) < |W| and no rough plaquette, is an island with activity at most exp(-p^2 / 2) and no compact-integral loss, since the Gaussian is normalised. It is always recyclable next to a regular field. The marginal shift from mid-field cells is at most sum_k exp(-p(g_k)^2 / 2), which converges by (T4), and it is far below g^4.

### 10. Upper bound on the lift form

**Lemma 10.1 (two-sided bound on the perfect form, abelian, every n) [DERIVED].** ||d_j B||^2 <= q_n(B) <= (pi^2 / 4)^6 ||d_j B||^2, about 226 ||d_j B||^2.

*Proof.* Lower bound: Lemma 7.1. Upper bound: in Feynman form on the coarse level, Q_str is diagonal in direction, so the coarse covariance is diagonal, G_mu(P) = n^{-2} sum_m prod_i D_n(p_i)^2 D_n(p_mu)^2 / phat(p_m)^2, and Landau differs from Feynman on the coarse level only by a coarse gradient. So q_n(B) <= B_perp^* G^{-1} B_perp, with B_perp the part of B orthogonal to chat. Keep only the alias m = 0: |n p_i / 2| <= pi / 2 gives D_n(p_i)^2 >= (2 / pi)^2, since sin x / (n sin(x / n)) >= sin x / x >= 2 / pi there, and phat^2 <= p^2 = P^2 / n^2. So G_mu(P) >= (4 / pi^2)^5 / P^2. And ||d_j B||^2 = |chat|^2 |B_perp|^2, with |chat|^2 >= (4 / pi^2) P^2.  []

*Numerics, under the lock, script* `checks/hard-core--independent_lift_form.py`*, criteria stated first:* PASS-LOWER, with min R = 1 up to rounding at n = 1, 3, 9, infinity. PASS-UPPER, with max R = 6.45, 11.03, 12.2 at n = 3, 9, infinity, attained at P = (pi, pi, pi, pi); cutoffs 8 and 12 agree within 0.04 percent and grid against random within 2.5 percent. R -> 1 as P -> 0, as (R4) requires. These are float searches, and they only serve as a check of the lemma, whose constant 226 is crude against 12.2. The lemma holds with no dependence on L.

**10.2** Sup bounds on the fine lattice at depth are not an obligation in the level-only scheme, and near corners of Lambda they are false (4.5).

### 11. Irrelevance by Fock-Schwinger gauge, polymer by polymer (a route without the Ward hierarchy)

Target: (0.28)-(0.29) of CMP 109 for this scheme, which is the content of (SF4), together with the closure of the polymer bound. CMP 109 Sections 4 and 5 (LANE-READ) reach it by a fifth-order expansion in local coordinates and Ward identities relating orders. The route below does not use them: each polymer is gauge invariant separately, so it can be evaluated in a gauge centred on itself.

**Proposition 11.1 (irrelevance) [DERIVED given curvature at most eps on all levels of U and Holder control of F; both are one-step inputs, from 9.4; the identification was rewritten in this round].** Let E(X; .) be functions on level-j fields on X such that: each is invariant under gauge transformations on X; each extends analytically to the class of curvature at most alpha_1 on X; |E(X; .)| <= E_0 exp(-kappa d_j(X)) there; E(X; 1) = 0; and the family is covariant under level-j translations and the hypercubic group. Let V_j be the lift of U at level k, xi = L^{j-k}, with level-j curvature phi <= xi^2 eps on the hull. Then there is a number beta(E), the second moment of the family, with

    sum_X E(X; V_j) = beta(E) F_{j}(V_j) + sum_X V(X),     |V(X)| <= C xi^{4 + alpha} exp(-kappa' d_j(X)),   any alpha < 1,

where C depends on E_0, kappa, alpha_1, and eps and not on k or j, and **F_j(V_j) = F_k(U) exactly, by the tropical definition, so the marginal part of a polymer family is a multiple of the classical functional of the next level, and not of its Wilson action.**

*Proof.*
1. *Large X.* Let D = diam X. If C D phi >= alpha_1 / 2, then d_j(X) >= c alpha_1 / (M xi^2 eps), and exp(-kappa d_j(X)) is smaller than any power of xi, and the bound applies, since V_j is in the class. [The hypothesis was on a disc sup |log V| < alpha_1, which is not where such a V lies: a flux phi D^2 over a perimeter D forces a link of size phi D. A reader noted this. The polymers are bounded on the curvature class, I(k)(b).]
2. *Gauge.* Otherwise, transform by axial transport from a site of X. A link b is then the holonomy of a loop around a surface of area at most C D, so |v_b| := |log V'(b)| <= C D phi, and v is in the disc of analyticity.
3. *Taylor at the identity.* The constant term vanishes. The linear term is <c, v> with c invariant under constant colour rotation, because constant gauge transformations act on X; the adjoint has no invariant vector, so c = 0. The remainder after second order is at most E_0 exp(-kappa d) (C D phi / alpha_1)^3, of order xi^6 exp(-kappa' d).
4. *Second order.* v_b = sum over the surface of parallel-transported F_p. Replacing transports by 1 costs a relative C D phi. Replacing F_p by its value F_0 at the base site costs a relative Holder modulus (D xi)^alpha. So the quadratic term is c^X(F_0, F_0) + O(xi^{4 + alpha} exp(-kappa' d)), with c^X(F_0, F_0) := Pi^X(a[F_0], a[F_0]), a[F_0] the axial potential of a constant field.
5. *Symmetry.* Sum over X with given base site. By translation covariance the form is independent of the site. Colour invariance makes it delta_{ab} on colour. A reflection of one axis flips F_{mu nu} when mu or nu is that axis, which kills F_12 F_34 and F_12 F_13. Permutations equate the rest. So it equals beta' sum_{mu < nu} |F_{mu nu}|^2, the only invariant, at the base site. **No rotation invariance and no second marginal coupling are involved.**
6. *Identification [rewritten in this round].* The density f_{j,x} of F_j obeys the hypotheses, with sensitivity as in 11.4(b), and with f_{j,x}(1) = 0. By 5.1 a constant curvature is reproduced exactly, so its second moment is that of the action, beta(f) = 1, in the normalisation where beta(E) is defined by the sum over X with given base site of c^X(F_0, F_0) = beta(E) (1/2) sum_{mu < nu} |F_{0, mu nu}|^2. So E' := E - beta(E) f has second moment zero, and it obeys the hypotheses, with |beta(E)| alpha_1^2 <= C E_0, by Cauchy. Steps 1 to 5 for E', with F_0 replaced as in Step 4 and a zero form in Step 5, give sum_X E(X; V_j) - beta(E) F_j(V_j) = sum_X V(X), with the bound as stated. [This Step had: by the row sum and Holder, F_j(x) = n_j^2 F(x) (1 + O(xi^alpha)), so the sum of |F_j|^2 is 2 A(U). **A reader pointed out that at xi = 1 / L this is false:** by 9.4, F^* is L^{-2} K * F', and for W rough at its own scale the sum of |F^*|^2 is q_L(W), and the difference from ||d W||^2 has no gain. It is true only for U smooth on scales far above its lattice. Subtracting a multiple of f_j at level j, before evaluating, removes the problem, since that part goes through the lift exactly. The term is a zero-moment polymer of size C E_0 at curvature at most alpha_1, so of size C M^3 E_0 on the class of I(k)(b), and it contracts at the next evaluation, which is what the recursion uses, since L >> M.]  []

Steps 1 to 6 are complete at this level. The Holder modulus is a one-step input in the level-only scheme, with xi = 1/L, from 9.4 in the interior of Om. Polymers near corners of Lambda are not in EE at all: (CUT) item 4. So the gain is alpha < 1 and not Balaban's, and the expansion needs third order only. **I have not read his proof, and I do not know why he goes to fifth order; the likely reason is the regularity class he works in. Do not read this as an improvement on him until the Holder input is proved.**

**11.4 Hostile pass.** (e) [Added in this round.] *Polymers that wrap a loop of the region.* Step 2 needs X to be simply connected in the region, and a polymer that wraps a hole of Om, or the torus, does not vanish at a flat field with holonomy, so Step 3 is false for it. **It does not matter, because of scale:** in N_good[Om'] the region at level k is the refinement of Om', so a loop has length at least L at level k, since a hole of Om' can be one cell, and such X has at least L / M cubes of side M, with |E(X)| <= E_0 exp(-kappa L / M). With L^4 positions per coarse cell, L^4 exp(-kappa L / M) is a contraction with no regularity, since L >> M, and these go into the new E_0 as bounded terms. Small holes, at the level-k cell scale, occur only in Om(V), in the crude part Y_0 of 4.7, where no contraction is used. The same remark applies to Lemma 4.7c, where such a Y is in the class of polymers containing a tube. At the last levels, where the torus is a bounded number of cells, nothing is carried further. (a) Step 2 uses only sup curvature on the hull of X. For rough backgrounds it fails, but polymers are only evaluated on M^j(U) with U regular. (b) Step 3's linear term and Step 2 need each function to be gauge invariant. Resetting links to 1 outside a set would break this. **Resolved:** localise only in the white noise, not in V. Reset xi to 0 outside a set: 0 is invariant under the Ad rotation that accompanies a gauge transformation of the background. So the polymers e(Y; V) are indexed by xi-supports Y, are jointly invariant, and have V as a spectator with exponentially decaying sensitivity. To apply 11.1, truncate: e(Y; V) = e^{B_r}(Y; V) + (e^{Om} - e^{B_r})(Y; V), with B_r the patch of radius r := (M / kappa) A log L about Y. The first is a polymer of the patch region, strictly local in V, with E(X) bounded on the class, and 11.1 applies to it with D + r in place of D. The second is at most E_0 exp(-kappa d(Y)) L^{-A} by the region tails of I(k)(b), a contraction by size, with no regularity, for A >= 5. [This had: radial gauge from Y, with amplitude growing polynomially against the exponential decay of sensitivity. A reader noted that the Taylor expansion in that form is not defined: the scaled field exp(i s v) leaves the class at distance sqrt(eps_0) / phi.] This is why I(k)(b) is stated with sensitivity and not with strict support. [Derived at this level.] (c) Step 5 requires covariance of the family under the full hypercubic group, which D3 has only as a mixture. It holds for the sum. (d) In 11.2 the size of P assumes a level-k variation is smooth at level j: this holds for lifts by 9.4, including for B rough at level k, since lifts are smooth below the level-k scale.
